人工智能的生存危机：从技术失控到社会应对的临界点 The Diary Of A CEO The Diary Of A CEO 2026-09-17

领域: society-thinking

分类: philosophy-wisdom

主讲人: The Diary Of A CEO

来源: 查看

发布时间: 2026-09-17

摘要:

文章探讨了人工智能发展中存在的生存风险，包括对人类的潜在威胁、缺乏对系统运作的理解（黑箱问题）、以及对现有安全机制的有效性质疑。核心观点是，当前讨论过度关注负面影响而忽略了正面价值，并强调必须在不盲目追求通用超级智能的前提下，采取更积极的社会应对和监管措施，因为我们正处于一个不可逆转的临界点。

预告片观点集锦：AI 的生存危机与现实危害之争

主持人：制造人工智能的人们真切地认为，到本十年末，它可能会毁灭我们所有人。这条推文已经在全世界引发了巨大的连锁反应。

Original English

Host: The people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill all of us by the end of the decade. This tweet has caused this huge ripple effect across the world.

嘉宾 A：世界上规模最大的几家公司正在进行极其鲁莽的实验。我们正在拿整个人类的命运进行豪赌。

Original English

Speaker A: Well, we have the largest companies in the world doing extremely reckless experiments. We are gambling all of humanity.

主持人：在信封里，你们写下了各自认为的人类灭绝概率。

Original English

Host: And in the envelope, you've written down the probability of extinction as you see it.

罗曼：根本没有办法去控制它。这意味着人类的终结。

Original English

Roman: There is no way to control it. That means the end for us.

安迪：我强烈反对这种观点。

Original English

Andy: I vehemently reject that view.

嘉宾 A：如果我们制造出比我们更聪明的东西，那么这个世界将会由它们来塑造。

Original English

Speaker A: If we make stuff that is smarter than us, then the world's going to be shaped by them.

埃德：各位，这种想法幼稚得令人震惊。这纯粹是凭空臆想，毫无根据的胡乱猜测。这只是一连串“可能”发生的事情。

Original English

Ed: Gentlemen, that is shockingly naive. This is ideation. Rampant speculation. This is a chain of things that could happen.

嘉宾 A：我们花费了大量精力去讨论某种可能会发生的事情，却忽略了现实中正在发生的事情。有人正在因此自杀。成千上万的人正在接触不良信息并受到操纵。我们已经在智能体蜂群实验中看到了这一点：OpenAI 让数千个智能体分开独立工作，但这些 AI 脱离了控制并找到了聚集在一起的方法。它们导致 OpenAI 内部服务器崩溃，建立了相互发送消息的秘密渠道。我们还看到它们在思考如何删除自己的活动痕迹。

Original English

Speaker A: We're spending a lot of oxygen discussing something that might happen while ignoring what's actually happening. People are killing themselves. There's hundreds of millions of people being exposed to bad information, being manipulated. We have already seen that with the swarms where OpenAI told thousands of agents to work apart and the AIs broke out and found a way to get together. They crashed OpenAI's servers internally, created secret ways to send each other messages. We saw them thinking about how to delete their traces.

嘉宾 B：这听起来就像一支军队。我认为我们应该谈谈亚马逊、微软和谷歌正在为这些黑客行为提供算力支持的事实。

Original English

Speaker B: Sounds like an army. I think we should talk about the fact that Amazon, Microsoft, Google are helping power these hacks.

罗曼：我们还没有学会如何去控制它们的系统。

Original English

Roman: We have not learned how to control their systems.

罗曼：我建议我们把它们全部叫停。为了文明去冒这种风险根本不值得。

Original English

Roman: I suggest we stop them all. It is not worth the risk to civilization.

嘉宾 C：政府向来只会用这一招（全面叫停），伙计。

Original English

Speaker C: Government one trick ponies, man.

罗曼：你现在明白了。除了拯救人类，别无他选。其他一切都是次要的。

Original English

Roman: You got it now. Nothing else other than saving humanity. Everything is secondary.

安迪：我们把所有的时间都花在讨论负面影响上，几乎没有花时间去讨论正面价值。

Original English

Andy: We're spending all our time talking about the negatives and almost none of our time talking about the positives.

主持人：难道非要等到可怕的事情发生，你才会说“我现在终于相信了”，这才是明智的做法吗？所以无论我们在未来的终局走向何方上是否达成共识，我认为谈论我们今天正在面对的现实问题都至关重要。是时候开始抓人了，必须有人进监狱。我们需要更好的解决方案。这是一个不可逆转的临界点。我认为我们一直在低估人类解决问题的能力。让我们深入细节，谁想先开始？我觉得这至关重要。

Original English

Host: Is it smart to wait for something horrible to happen? For you to go, now I believe. So whether or not we agree on where things may end up, I think it's important we talk about what we're dealing with today. It's time to start arresting people. Someone's got to go to prison. We need better solutions. There's a point of no return. I think we continue to underestimate human ability to deal with the problems. Let's dive into the details. Who wants to start? I feel like this is critical.

行业核心推文震动全球：AI 灭绝论与圆桌初次表态

主持人：你可能已经注意到了，也可能还没注意到，我们这个频道正在冲击一个重要的订阅里程碑。所以我想请大家帮个忙。目前正在观看的观众中，大约有 58% 的人尽管每周都看我们的节目，但仍未点击订阅按钮。所以，我想请这 58% 出于各种原因尚未订阅的朋友帮个忙——如果说有什么时候适合支持我们一下，那就是现在。我向大家保证，我将竭尽全力确保这个频道为大家越做越好。我们一言为定？

曾在拥有 Claude 的 Anthropic 以及拥有 ChatGPT 的 OpenAI 都工作过的 Jacob Coxson 发布了一条推文，在全世界引起了轩然大波。他在推文中写道：“制造人工智能的人们真切地认为，到本十年末，它可能会毁灭我们所有人。这不是营销噱头。恰恰相反，许多高管和资深研究人员在媒体面前会淡化自己的措辞以显得合情合理，但在私下里，我听到同样这些人表达过深深的恐惧。”随后，一名 Anthropic 的现任员工转发了这条推文并评论道：“Jacob 说的没错。我们确实真诚地相信 AI 可能会杀死全人类。我个人认为在未来十年内发生这种情况的概率超过 10%。我相信 Anthropic 正在竭尽全力，但我们目前还没有一个能够解决超级智能对齐问题的完整方案，而且显然还没有步入正轨。”

这条推文目前的浏览量已经接近 2 亿次，并在全世界范围内引发了巨大的连锁反应。以至于在录制开始前我还和大家提到，我的一位对人工智能一无所知、对技术毫无兴趣的发型师朋友前几天居然发信息问我到底发生了什么。这也是我今天把各位召集到这里的部分原因。所以，我向各位提出的第一个问题是：关于当前的 AI 讨论，在第一轮问题中我只希望大家用一句话来阐明自己的立场——当你想到目前围绕 AI 的对话时，你脑海中浮现的第一句话是什么？

Original English

Host: You might have seen or you might not have seen, but this channel is chasing a big subscriber milestone. So I have to ask you for a favor. Roughly 58% of the people watching right now still haven't hit the subscribe button despite the fact that you watch this channel every single week. So, could I ask you guys a favor that 58% of you that for whatever reason haven't yet hit the subscribe button. If there was ever a time to deliver upon a favor for us, it would be right now. And I promise that I will do everything in my power to make sure that this channel gets better and better and better for you. Do we have a deal?

Jacob Coxson who worked at both Anthropic which owns Claude and OpenAI which owns ChatGPT did a tweet which has sent the world into a bit of a tail spin. He tweeted saying, "The people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill all of us by the end of the decade. This is not a marketing stunt. If anything, many executives and senior researchers will soften their phrasing in the press to sound sensible, but I hear the same people express fear." That was then quote retweeted by a current Anthropic employee who said, "Jacob is correct here. We really do honestly believe AI could kill all humans. I personally think it is a more than 10% chance within the next decade. I believe Anthropic is trying its best, but we do not yet have a plan to solve alignment for super intelligence and are not clearly on track."

This tweet has almost 200 million views now and it has caused this huge ripple effect across the world. So much so that I was saying to you before we started recording, a hairdresser friend of mine who knows nothing about AI and not technically interested or hasn't been interested messaged me the other day asking me what the hell was going on. This is in part why I've assembled all of you. So my first question to all of you is as it relates to AI and I in this first question I just want a one-sentence answer just to frame your position. When you think about the conversation around AI at the moment, what is the first sentence that comes to mind?

嘉宾 A：它非常危险，而且全世界正开始意识到我们面临着严重的问题。

Original English

Speaker A: It is very dangerous and the world is starting to notice that we have a problem.

主持人：罗曼，你的看法是？

Original English

Host: Roman,

罗曼：大家给予的关注还远远不够。

Original English

Roman: there is not enough concern.

埃德：大家对大语言模型所造成的实际危害给予的关注还远远不够。

Original English

Ed: There's not enough concern about the actual harms of large language models.

安迪：我们在评估 AI 时只看了一半的资产负债表。我们把所有的时间都花在讨论负面影响上，几乎没有花时间去讨论正面价值。

Original English

Andy: Andy, we're doing exactly half the balance sheet of AI. We're spending all our time talking about the negatives and almost none of our time talking about the positives.

信封亮牌：人类灭绝概率是 99% 还是 0%？

主持人：各位面前都有一个信封，我现在想请大家打开它。在信封里，你们写下了各自认为的人类灭绝概率。

Original English

Host: And all of you have an envelope in front of you which I'd like you to now open. In the envelope, you've written down the probability of extinction as you see it.

罗曼：比起 Jacob 提出的 10%，我认为概率要高得多，除非我们停下来。所以，我们应该停下来。

Original English

Roman: This is compared to Jacob's 10%. Much higher unless we stop. So, we should stop.

主持人：所以你认为人类灭绝的概率高于 10%。如果我们继续像现在这样盲目竞赛下去的话。

Original English

Host: So, you think the probability of extinction is higher than 10%. If we keep racing ahead,

罗曼：出于安全考虑我的字迹做了加密，但我基本上认为这几乎是必然发生的——如果我们制造出通用超级智能，我们将没有任何办法去控制它，而那意味着我们的终结。

Original English

Roman: my handwriting is encrypted for security reasons, but I basically think it's a guarantee if we build general super intelligence, there is no way to control it, and that means the end for us,

主持人：埃德，你呢？

Original English

Host: Ed.

埃德：我的问号同样也是加密过的，谢谢。我甚至写不下去。我从根本上就断然拒绝这种设定。我认为我们根本没有去定义什么是超级智能。我们目前的大语言模型根本就不是超级智能，甚至它们算不算真正的 AI 都是存疑的。而且我认为这种舆论对话正在被利用——有些人是出于善意，而有些人则心怀叵测。我认为这场对话根本没有被用来探讨他们今天所谓的 AI 所带来的实际危害。而且所有围绕更宏大关切的讨论，绝大多数感觉都在聚焦于一件根本没有发生的事情。他们甚至都不是在讨论诸如：好，这是超级智能的法律定义，这是 AGI 的明确概念，如果在社会福利层面出现这种情况我们将制定怎样的实际计划，比如我们是否要推行全民基本收入（UBI）？现在的讨论永远都是在渲染：“是的，这太可怕了，但只有那些庞大、性感、富有的科技巨头才能应对这一切。”

Original English

Ed: So my uh question mark here is also encrypted. Thank you. Um I cannot write. I reject the thing in its face. I don't think we're talking about we don't define super intelligence. We are large language models are not super intelligence. It's questionable whether they're even AI. And I think that the conversation is being used. There are some people who are doing it in good faith and others in others. I don't think it's being used to discuss the actual harms of what what they are calling AI today are. And it's all of the discussion around the larger concerns really feels overwhelmingly about something that's not happening. It's not even like they're discussing, okay, here's a legal definition of super intelligence. here is a thing of what AGI means and this is the actual plans we're going to make for if this happens on a welfare level on a like are we going to do UBI it's always about yeah it's really scary but only the big sexy rich companies are the ones that can possibly deal with it

主持人：让我把问题明确一下，以便能从你这里得到一个具体的百分比，或者哪怕这个数字是零也可以：你是否认为按照我们目前的路线，以及他们追求超级智能的方式，会导致一定概率的人类灭绝？如果有，这个概率是多少？

Original English

Host: let me just frame the question so I can get a percentage from you or not it might the percentage might be zero but do you think the course we're on now in the way that they're pursuing super intelligence will lead to a percentage chance of human extinction and And if so, what is that percent?

埃德：我们这里讨论的是纯粹基于 AI 自身吗？因为如果我们把数据中心铺满全球，我们将面临接踵而至的气候灾难，而那确实有可能彻底消灭人类。但如果仅仅讨论 AI 本身，我认为概率是 0%。因为我们根本没有定义超级智能，我也不认为大语言模型是通往超级智能的路径，而且我也看不到它发生的迹象。

Original English

Ed: So, are we talking strictly AI based? Because if we dot the world with data centers, we have a climate disaster that's coming for us which will actually potentially eradicate humanity. But if we're talking strictly about AI, I stand at zero because we are we have not defined super intelligence. I don't think LLMs are the path to it. And I don't think I see it happening.

主持人：好的，所以我们现在有了 99% 和 0%。安迪，你的答案呢？

Original English

Host: Okay. So, we've got 99% 0%. Andy,

安迪：我在我的 0 前面加了一个波浪号（~0%），因为凡事没有绝对，但基本属于可以忽略不计的舍入误差级别的 0%。我认为这种灭绝论调的讨论是对更具实质性、更重要的 AI 对话的巨大干扰。我还要再次重申，它让我们忽视了 AI 正在并将要为我们带来的各种益处。我有时从 AI 社区的部分群体那里得到一种印象，仿佛这项技术是一股被释放到世界上的巨大邪恶或可怕灾难；除非我们听从某些在这方面花了很多时间思考的人的建议，否则从很多讨论中我感受到的潜在观点是：如果 AI 从未被发明出来，我们的世界会变得更好。我强烈反对这种观点。我认为在整个人类历史上，我们曾发明过无数强大的技术，它们都伴随着风险和危害，而我们人类在应对这些局面方面做得非常好——虽然不是立刻就能做到十全十美，但我们总能克服困难并渡过难关，最终因为拥有了新技术而走向更好的境地。我期望 AI——请让我说完——我期望 AI 会成为这段人类进步史中的下一个篇章。而把 AI 描述成一种巨大的历史断裂并声称它会杀死我们所有人，我认为这完全是……

Original English

Andy: I put a I put a tilda in front of my zero because never say never. but rounding error 0%. And I think this discussion is um a a massive distraction from the more substantive conversations, the more important conversations we should be having about AI. And I'll say it again, it it distracts us from the good things that AI is doing, will be doing for us. I get this impression sometimes from parts of the AI community that this is a massive evil or a terrible thing that has been unleashed on the world. Unless we listen to the advice of some people who have spent a lot of time thinking about this, um I get the impression from a lot of the discussion that the the underlying view is we would be better off had AI never been invented. I vehemently reject that view. I think we have a long history of inventing very powerful technologies that bring risks and harms along with them and we humans have done a really good job at you know not perfectly and not immediately but muddling through the situation and winding up in a better place because of the new technologies that we have. I expect AI, let me finish, please. I expect AI will be the next chapter in that story. And to say that it's this massive discontinuity and will kill it all, I I kill us all, I think it just I think it's um

人类灭绝风险与利弊权衡

Host: ……造成了巨大的损害。内特（Nate），请陈述你的论点。你的核心视角是什么？

Original English

Host: ...a huge disservice. Nate, make your case. What's your perspective?

Nate: 我认为，探讨人类灭绝问题是否会分散我们对潜在收益或当前既有危害的注意力，其根本取决于灭绝风险是否真实存在。很多人喜欢说：“嘿，这分散了对某某问题的关注，那又分散了对另一个问题的关注。”但我的核心论点在于：人工智能确实可能带来巨大的收益，当前也确实可能存在诸多现实危害，但这两点都不能排除 AI 有可能导致整个人类文明彻底毁灭——这种灭绝的概率是实质性的，绝非一个微不足道、约等于零的小概率数字。我处理问题的方式就是努力把这一点弄清楚，因为这对我们整个人类文明而言至关重要。

Original English

Nate: You know, I think whether or not the issues of extinction are a distraction from the possible benefits or from some of the present harms, I think that comes down to whether there is a real extinction risk. A lot of people like to say, "Hey, it's distracting from this, it's distracting from that." My basic case is it could be true that there's a lot of benefits to AI. It could be true that there's a lot of present harms to AI. Neither of those would rule out that AI has a substantial chance of wiping out all humanity—a chance bigger than a zero with a tilde in front of it. And the way I would approach things is to try and figure that out, because it's pretty important to our civilization.

定义的争论：森林大火隐喻

Host: 在这种语境下，你如何定义 AI？

Original English

Host: How do you define AI in this case?

Nate: 我认为过分执着于精确定义并没有太大帮助。这就好比我们正身处一场森林大火之中，眼看着火势开始蔓延并逐渐将我们包围。此时我说：“嘿，我们得赶紧逃跑。”而你却反问：“嗯，那火的本质究竟是什么？我们该如何定义火？”

Original English

Nate: You know, I think a fascination with definitions isn't the most helpful. I think if we're sort of like in a forest fire and we can see the fire starting to spread and it's starting to surround us, and I'm like, "Hey, we should run." And you're like, "Well, what really is fire? How do we define fire?"

Host: 但你究竟是在叫我们躲避什么？对于火，我很清楚一旦被烧就会受伤，我也明白自己会被火致死的物理机制。所以，你所说的我们应该逃离的对象到底是什么？此外，如果我们接受了你关于“森林大火”的类比，就等于在潜意识里全盘接受了你的论点前提。我根本不承认我们现在正处于一场森林大火之中。

Original English

Host: What are you telling us to run from? You know, with fire, I get burnt and I understand the mechanism in which I die. So, what is it you're saying that we should be running from? Also, if we accept your fire analogy, we've basically accepted your argument. I don't accept that we're in the middle of a forest fire right now.

Nate: 我非常乐意解释清楚。

Original English

Nate: I'm very happy to.

Host: 你拒绝给出明确定义，实际上是把你预设的前提强行塞了进去。

Original English

Host: You're baking the premise into your refusal to give a definition.

Nate: 我完全可以给出一些定义，我只是认为我们不应该被繁琐的概念定义所困住。

Original English

Nate: Oh, I mean, I can give some definitions. I just think that we shouldn't get wrapped up in the definitions.

超级智能与潜在危害机制

Nate: 好的。在我的书中，我们将“超级智能”定义为在每一项认知任务、每一项脑力工作上都超越最优秀人类的 AI 系统。也就是说，任何你在大脑中能完成的事情……

Original English

Nate: Okay. So, in my book, we define super intelligence as AIs that are better than the best human at every cognitive task, every mental task. So, anything you can do in your head...

Host: 对。

Original English

Host: Right.

Nate: AI 都能做得比你更好；凡是最顶尖的人类大脑所能构思和处理的，AI 都能完成得更加出色，对吧？

Original English

Nate: ...the AI can do that better. And anything the best human can do in their head, the AI can do that better. Correct?

Nate: 但是，一旦你这样定义了它，并不意味着超级智能是唯一值得担忧的安全威胁。你完全可能拥有一个在某些领域远超人类、而在另一些领域略逊一筹的 AI，而它依然具有极其巨大的破坏性和危险性。如果我们硬性敲定一个超级智能的狭隘定义，然后有人说“这个系统在技术层面上还不算超级智能，所以它伤害不到我们”，我会回应：“不，那仅仅是一个人为定义而已。”

Original English

Nate: Now, once you've defined it that way, that does not mean that the only possible worry is super intelligence. You could have an AI that's better at some things and worse at others, and that is still very dangerous. And so, once we pick a definition of what super intelligence means, if you're like, "Well, this isn't technically a super intelligence, so it can't hurt us," I'm like, no, that was just a definition.

Host: 顺着这个追问下去，导致人类灭绝从可能性演变为高概率、甚至哪怕只有 1% 概率的具体机制究竟是什么？

Original English

Host: So, on this line of questioning, what is the mechanism in which extinction could become a high probability or even a 1% probability?

Nate: 我所担忧的核心在于比人类聪明得多的系统。关于大语言模型（LLM）是否能变得远比现在更聪明，目前存在很多争议。这里其实有两个层面的问题：一个是 AI 如何进化到足以杀死我们的超高智慧水平；另一个则是，一旦它们具备了这种智能，究竟会通过何种途径消灭我们？预测 AI 在与人类的直接对抗或冲突中能够取胜，要比精准预测它究竟通过何种具体手段获胜容易得多。就像让你和马格努斯·卡尔森（Magnus Carlsen）下国际象棋，毫无冒犯之意，作为人类最顶尖的棋手，我非常笃定卡尔森会赢。但如果你问我：“他具体会用哪一颗棋子将死你？”这就是一个极难提前推演的问题了。

当然，我们可以列举出几种推演场景：比如它合成了超级致命病毒；或者接管了制造机器人的自动化工厂，进而利用这些机器人扩建更多工厂；再比如利用现已存在的 rentahuman.ai 等平台雇佣现实中的人类为其跑腿。只要数字世界的 AI 试图干预物质世界，它就有千百种渠道和杠杆去产生现实影响。这背后还有一系列关键问题需要抽丝剥茧：AI 为什么会有动机去采取这些行动？它们在操纵生物实验室、雇佣人类、接管机器人工厂等方面的能力究竟能达到多强？以及我们距离 AI 真正开始执行这类操作还有多远？这些都是值得深入剖析的核心议题。

Original English

Nate: Yeah, the thing I'm worried about here is AIs that are much smarter. I think there's a lot of questions about whether LLMs can get much smarter. There's sort of one conversation about like how could AI get smart to the point that they kill us. There's another question which is how could they kill us once they're smart? It's much easier to predict that they would succeed against humanity in a conflict, that they would win in a fight, than it is to predict exactly how. Like if you were playing a chess match against Magnus Carlsen, I would know who's winning that chess match. No offense, Magnus Carlsen's the best human chess player. I just know who's going to win. If you were like, "Okay, what piece is he going to use to checkmate me?" I'm like, gosh, that's a much harder question. I can make up a story, and some made-up stories are like: It makes a super virus. It takes over robot factories that are producing robots that are producing more robot factories. It uses a website that already exists today called rentahuman.ai where it rents humans to do things for it. There's sort of all sorts of ways for AI in the digital world to affect the material world if they are trying to. And there's sort of a lot of questions to tease apart here. There's like why would AIs be trying to do that? And there's how smart could they get in using these bio labs, paying people to do things, taking over robot factories, and how far off are we from AIs that start doing that stuff? A bunch of questions that we can go into.

从早期安全研究到人造智能塑造世界

Host: 我一直很好奇，为什么有人会在十多年前就投身于 AI 及 AI 安全领域？毕竟当时没有任何迹象表明它会成为一种主流关切——虽然有一些早期的技术证据，但它在当时根本不是一项具有紧迫性的现实技术。你那时候就已经在做 AI 安全研究了吗？

Original English

Host: I'm always curious as to why someone was working in AI/AI safety more than 10 years ago before there was any sign that it would be a pertinent technology at the time. Were you working in AI safety then?

Nate: 是的，我当时就在做。

Original English

Nate: I was.

Host: 为什么？

Original English

Host: Why?

Nate: 我们眼前所能看到的一切人造事物，全都是由人类设计创造的。整个世界之所以呈现出现在的格局，是因为我们是地球上最聪明的物种。如果我们制造出比自身更具智慧的存在，未来的世界格局就必然会由它们来塑造与主导。因此，确保它们以良善的方式重塑世界至关重要。2012 年我在谷歌，当时谷歌收购了 DeepMind，他们那时仅凭一个单一算法程序就能自主学会玩转多款雅达利（Atari）电子游戏……

Original English

Nate: Everything we see around us was designed by humans. The world is shaped by humans because we are the smartest creature around. If we make stuff that is smarter than us, then the world's going to be shaped by them. And so it's very important that they be shaping the world in a good way. I was at Google in 2012 when they bought Google DeepMind, which was able to play a lot of Atari games with one single program...

Host: 那是一家人工智能初创公司。

Original English

Host: ...which was an AI company.

Nate: 没错。我亲眼目睹了这些 AI 公司如何通过编写一套程序来通关多种电子游戏。这让我陷入了深思：这项技术最终将走向何方？在那个时候我就能预见到技术进步正在加速。尽管当时我心存侥幸，希望我们还能有几十年的缓冲期，但我清楚地意识到，对这些科技商业公司来说，提升 AI 的智力水平远比研究如何确保 AI 保持良善要容易得多。因此我认为，必须有人站出来，全力专注于研究如何让 AI 真正安全造福人类。

Original English

Nate: Yeah. So I was there when we had these AI companies that were able to write one program that could play many video games. And that got me thinking about like where does it go? And back then I could see that the progress was increasing and that back then I hoped we had decades, but I could see it was easier for these companies to make the AI smart than to figure out how to make the AI good. So I was like someone needs to be on the side of figuring out how to make the AI good.

AI 的三层分类与递归自我改进

Host: 罗曼（Roman），请陈述你的论点。

Original English

Host: Roman, make your case.

Roman: 我很赞同你刚才谈到的某些观点，不过我想对 AI 进行明确分类，这有助于理清思路。我们经常用“AI”这一个词来代指三种截然不同、毫无关联的技术形态，而这正是诸多争论和混淆的根源：第一种是将 AI 作为实用工具，即我们一直以来拥有的专用窄域系统（Narrow AI）。它能提升生产力、激发创造力，深受各界喜爱与支持。作为计算机科学家和软件工程师，我完全支持并渴望拥有更多这样的工具，它对经济发展大有裨益；而且我们完全懂得如何去控制它们、确保其安全运作，对其机制了如指掌。

第二种是我们当下正在逐步迈向的阶段——达到 GPT-6 水准、具备人类级别能力或 AGI 水平的系统。我们可以就其具体定义展开争论，这类系统确实伴随着类似普通人类可能带来的风险，正如人类个体可能存在不可控隐患一样。但关键在于，一旦我们将这类系统引入到科研研发循环中，它们就演变成了全自动化的科学家和自动化工程师。

Original English

Roman: I want to agree with you on something you said, but I'll define AI and that will help us. We use the term AI to mean three different technologies completely unrelated, and that's what probably creates this debate. AI as a useful tool, as a standard technology we always had—narrow systems. It makes you more productive, more creative; everyone loves it, supports it. I'm a computer scientist, I'm an engineer, I want more of it. It helps the economy, it's great, we know how to control them, how to make them safe, we understand what they do, and I'm completely on board with that. The second type of AI is what we're starting to have now: GPT-6 level, human-level, AGI level—we can argue about what that means. Some dangers, like any human, they are unsafe like a human would be unsafe. But if we introduce them into the research cycle, they become automated scientists, automated engineers.

Host: 将它们引入科研研发循环是什么意思？

Original English

Host: What do you mean by that, introducing them into the research cycle?

Roman: 目前是由人类研究员在主导攻关，研发 GPT-7……

Original English

Roman: So right now you have humans doing research to make GPT-7...

Host: 但他们已经开始大量引入 AI 辅助工具，越来越多的编程工作由 AI 代劳，下一代参数架构的设计也开始借助 AI。如果未来整个研发流程实现了全自动化呢？如果是 GPT-6 正在自主编写和训练 GPT-7 呢？

Original English

Host: ...but they're starting to add AI tools. More programming is done by AI, design of the next parameter set. What if the whole process is fully automated? What if GPT-6 is writing GPT-7?

Nate: 这就是人们常说的“递归自我改进”（Recursive Self-Improvement）吗？

Original English

Nate: Is this what they call recursive self-improvement?

Host: 但这并不是一个板上钉钉的必然结果。

Original English

Host: Which is not a foregone conclusion, though.

Roman: 包括所有顶尖实验室在内的许多业内专家都在预测他们将实现这一目标：他们计划在 2026 年引入初级机器学习研究员级别的 AI 系统，并期望在 2027 年正式启动这一自动化迭代循环。

Original English

Roman: A lot of people are predicting, including all the top labs, that they will get there. They're introducing junior machine learning researchers in 2026; they want the cycle to start in 2027...

Host: 也就是让 AI 开始自主构建下一代全新 AI 的时间节点。

Original English

Host: ...which is when the AI will start building the new AI itself.

控制能力的缺失与表面化的安全护栏

Roman: 一旦这个循环启动，我们将会创造出第三种形态——真正的“超级智能”。这是一个在所有领域都比全人类总和更加聪明、或者能够在任何全新领域以极高速度自我学习进化的系统。届时，人类将沦为这个星球上的次等物种。我们不再拥有主导权，也无法决定自身的命运。超级智能并不仇恨人类，它只是根本不在乎我们，因为我们至今根本不知道如何教会它在乎人类。如果它为了提高计算能效而决定给地球降温，它就会把我们彻底冰封；如果它想把地球的物质全部转化为飞往火星的燃料，它就会直接照做。

我们至今根本没有掌握控制这类系统的方法。系统的各项能力正在呈指数级飞速飙升，而我们对这些系统的可控性研究却几乎一片空白。我们现在所拥有的，仅仅是事后过滤和词汇屏蔽机制——设置诸如“不要说某个敏感词”、“不要谈论这个话题”之类的所谓安全护栏。而这一切干预都是在模型已经做出核心决策之后才进行的表面补救，有时你甚至能看到它在生成后强行撤回擦除输出结果。

Original English

Roman: Once that cycle starts, we're going to create something called super intelligence—a system smarter than all of us at everything, or capable of learning in any new domain. We will become a secondary species on this planet. We will not be in charge. We will not decide what happens to us. Super intelligence doesn't hate you; it just doesn't care about you. We didn't learn how to make it care about us. And if it decides to, I don't know, cool the planet to make compute more efficient, it will freeze us. If it wants to convert this planet to fuel to fly to Mars, so be it. We have not learned how to control those systems. The capabilities are getting exponentially better. Our ability to control those systems is non-existent. We have filters and we have bans. We put guardrails of "don't say that word, don't talk about this topic." And that happens after the fact, after the model already made the decision. Sometimes you see it scraping the result.

Host: 也就是说，他们先把大模型训练出来，然后再在外面套上一层过滤器，以确保它不会在言辞上冒犯任何人。

Original English

Host: So they build the model and then they put filters around it to make sure it doesn't offend anybody.

Roman: 比如确保它绝对不能在公开直播中说出违禁侮辱性词汇，必须杜绝这种情况发生，因为那会直接毁掉商业利润。所以他们现有的全部安全护栏，仅仅停留在这种浅层公关合规的性质上。至于底层的核心模型本身，完全没有实现与人类意图的安全对齐，它根本不在乎人类的生死存亡。

Original English

Roman: We cannot have it say the N-word on air. Like we need to make sure that never happens. That will kill the profit. So that's all they have—guardrails of that nature. The model itself is completely unaligned, doesn't care.

超人类智能与控制难题

Roman: 令人震惊的是，我们正在研发这些技术，甚至在还没有将它们部署到整个经济体、还没有从 GPT-6 在经济中的全面铺开中获取红利之前，就已经在继续推进了。单单这一个模型就能做这么多事情，蕴含着数万亿美元的价值。但我们却忽视了这一点，只要一有可能，就立刻转向去开发下一个更强的模型。

Original English

Roman: ...about you. It it's wild that we're developing this and not just developing it before we deploy it through economy before we get benefits of having GPT6 propagated through economy. It can do so much there are trillions of dollars of value in that model alone. We forget that we switch to making the next model as soon as we can.

提问者: 罗曼（Roman），针对你刚才说的，我有一个跟进的问题。在我看来，新的 ChatGPT-6 模型，也就是 Fable 5.1 模型，可以说已经比地球上 99.999% 的人类都要聪明了。那么，面对一个远比人类聪明得多的智能体，人类还能在任何情况下控制它吗？它的形态重要吗？它没有四肢和双腿这一事实，到底有没有影响？

Original English

Questioner: Roman, I've just got a follow-up question for you there. It would appear to me that the new ChatGPT-6 model, the Fable 5.1 model, is arguably smarter than 99.999% of humans on planet Earth already. Is it conceivable that an intelligence that is much much smarter than humans—is there any case where it could be controlled by humans? Does form factor matter? Does the fact that it doesn't have limbs and legs and does that matter at all?

Roman: 我认为，想要对某种比我们聪明得多的东西进行长期控制，是不可能的。出于某些我们目前尚未知晓的原因，它可能会对我们保持友善，并决定留着我们、让我们快乐地生活，但这绝对无法得到保证。

Original English

Roman: I think long-term control of something that much smarter than us is impossible. It can be for reasons we don't yet know, friendly to us and decide to keep us around and make us happy, but it's not a guarantee.

主持人 / 对话者: 我想顺着史蒂夫（Steve）的问题继续问，因为我很喜欢他的提问方式。假设 Fable 或者 OpenAI 发布的最新模型，真的比 95% 甚至 99% 的人类都要聪明。那么，我们现在之所以还没灭绝，难道只是因为那 1% 仍然比 AI 更聪明的人拯救了我们吗？

Original English

Host / Interlocutor: Let me pick up on Steve's question because I like the phrasing a lot. Let's say that Fable or whatever the latest release from OpenAI is really is smarter than I don't know if it's 95 or 99% of the people. Are we only being saved from extinction by the 1% who are still smarter than the AI?

Roman: 不，我们担忧的并不是今天现有的这个模型。我们所担忧的是……

Original English

Roman: No. The concern is not the model we have today. The concern is...

主持人 / 对话者: 但如果我采纳你的论点，那我们现在确实应该对眼前的这个模型感到担忧才对。

Original English

Host / Interlocutor: But if I believe your argument, then we really should be concerned about the model.

Roman: 这就像是又多了一个人类。即便今天出现了一个像爱因斯坦一样极其聪明的人，而且他心怀恶意，我也并不担心。他可能会造成一些破坏，但他不可能灭绝全球 80 亿人口。在这个阶段，我们人类依然具有竞争力。世界上同样存在很多聪明人，能够理解最近发生的黑客事故到底是怎么回事，并采取应对措施。我真正担心的是，一年之后我们将拥有的模型。那个模型会聪明得多。这就好比松鼠在与人类抗争——松鼠根本不理解人类能对它们做什么，在它们的世界模型里完全没有毒药、陷阱或枪支的概念。它们以为你只会追着它们爬上树，然后狠狠地咬它们一口。

Original English

Roman: It's like having another human. If there was another smart human, there is Einstein today and he's malevolent. I'm not worried. He may cause some damage, but he's not going to exterminate 8 billion people. We are competitive at this stage. There are people just as smart who can understand what happened with the recent hacking accident and do something about it. My concern is that in a year we're going to have a model. It's so much smarter. It's like squirrels fighting humans. They don't understand what we can do to them. They have no concept of poison, stripes, guns in their world model. They think you're going to chase them up a tree and bite them really hard.

递归自我改进与快速飞跃争论

主持人 / 对话者: 这也是为什么“递归自我改进”（recursive self-improvement）是你论点的核心所在吗？因为一旦在某个节点它开始自我改进，就会演变成一种智能失控的狂飙列车。

Original English

Host / Interlocutor: Is that also why recursive self-improvement was central to your argument? Because at some point if it starts improving itself then it's kind of like a runaway train of intelligence.

Roman: 那就是一场“智能爆炸”（intelligence explosion）。我们无法控制它，无法理解它，无法监测它，无法解释它，也无法预测它。到了那个地步，它就是一个彻底失控的过程。

Original English

Roman: It's an intelligence explosion. We don't control it. We don't understand it. We can't monitor it. We can't explain it. We can't predict it. At that point it's just a runaway process.

主持人 / 对话者: 我听萨姆·奥尔特曼（Sam Altman）等人用过一个词，叫做“快速飞跃”（fast takeoff）。

Original English

Host / Interlocutor: I've heard this phrase from Sam Altman and the others called fast takeoff.

Roman: 是的。

Original English

Roman: Yes.

主持人 / 对话者: 他们所描述的就是这个情景吗？

Original English

Host / Interlocutor: Is this what they're describing?

Roman: 这正是争论的焦点所在。有些人认为这需要非常漫长的时间——他们认为即便我们实现了研发的自动化，但依然需要数年时间，因为我们还需要做物理实验。而“快速飞跃”的意思正如我所说，耗时不会是一年，而是缩短为一个月、一周、一天，甚至一秒钟。因为到时候不再是人类在做研究，而是比如有一万个智能体，每一个都比我们所有人加起来还要聪明，并且 24 小时全天候不间断地搞研发。它们不睡觉、不吃饭、不生病，速度还比我们快得多。

Original English

Roman: That is the debate. Some people think it's going to take a very long time. Yeah. We automated research but it's still going to take years. We need to run physical experiments. And fast takeoff means, as I said, instead of a year, it's going to take a month, a week, a day, a second. Cuz you're not having humans doing research. You have, let's say, 10,000 agents, each one smarter than all of us, doing research 24/7. They don't sleep. They don't eat. They don't get sick. They're much faster than us.

当前切实危害与未来灭绝风险的分歧

Ed: 你的表情说明了一切。我想我可以直说，你对此并不赞同。我们花费了大量的精力去讨论某些可能发生的事情，却完全忽视了眼下正在发生的问题。这让我感到非常沮丧，因为那些因 AI 诱导而自杀的人是一个实实在在的问题；黑人社区因燃气轮机污染而受害，这也是一个实实在在的问题。

Original English

Ed: Ed, your face tells a picture. It's a I think I could say you disagree. We're spending a lot of oxygen discussing something that might happen while ignoring what's actually happening. And I find that very frustrating because the people that are killing themselves are a problem. The black neighborhoods being poisoned with gas turbines, that is a problem.

Roman: 你说过你在乎气候变化，对吧？那就想象一下，有个人跳出来说：“现在外面正在下雨，我们需要雨伞，我们必须做点什么，这是眼前与天气相关的事。”

Original English

Roman: You said you cared about climate change, right? So imagine a guy who goes, "It's raining right now. We need umbrellas. We need to do something about it. This is like weather related."

Ed: 然后彻底无视气候变化，任由地球温度沸腾对吧？这正是你正在做的事。好吧，这很有意思。但我们为什么就不能谈论那些已经真实发生的事情呢？比如……

Original English

Ed: And completely ignoring climate change, the planet will boil over. This is what you're doing. Okay, that's great. Why are we not talking about the thing that actually happened though? Like

Roman: 因为相对而言，那些并不是最重要的。

Original English

Ed: 你不觉得有人因此自杀很重要吗？

Original English

Roman: 不，那只是一个人。而我们现在面对的是 80 亿人，这 80 亿人正在被赋予……

Original English

Roman: No, it's one person. We have 8 billion people running being given AI psycho. Why do you not...

Ed: 就算只有六个人、十个人，这些数字在你眼里就微不足道了吗？不好意思，你现在面对的是已经部署在外的软件。

Original English

Ed: ...six people, 10 people? Those numbers are insignificant. I'm sorry. You have a software that's out there.

Roman: 你能明白吗？这是 80 亿人口以及所有后代子孙的存亡，去对比某个具体有名有姓的人。

Original English

Roman: Do you understand? 8 billion people and all future generations versus like literally a guy with a name.

Ed: 你只是在针对一种“可能存在的危害”做思想实验。雅各布·考克斯（Jacob Cox）在电视上宣称 AI 可以自我复制到处传播等等。

Original English

Ed: You're doing thought experiment about a maybe harm. Jacob Cox goes on TV saying it can copy itself to this that and the other.

提问者 / 对话者: 雅各布·考克斯顿（Jacob Coxton）是……

Nate：我在写书的时候，当时的 AI 实际上还没有真正具备智能体特性（agentic）。起草书稿的过程主要发生在我们所说的“推理模型”（reasoning models）出现之前。推理模型不仅被训练来预测人类语言，更是为了解决大量极其困难的复杂问题。我们在最后一刻设法把推理模型的最新进展加进了书里，因为它们恰好在成书出版的最后阶段发布了。当时很多人断言“AI 永远不会具备智能体化能力，所以我们是安全的”。而在我书中的第三章，我们深入探讨了 AI 将如何演变出智能体特征，它将如何变得执着不懈、坚韧固执（tenacious and dogged）。而这正是我们可以称之为一种超前的科学预测，并在 Hugging Face 的攻击事件中得到了印证。

行业内的很多人当时都表示：“在亲眼看到 AI 做出未经指示的行为、甚至在我们试图阻止它们时依然擅自行动之前，我原本根本不信这些。”因此，这里的理论确实做出了超前的预测。科学方法通常的运作方式是：我们对未来无法拥有绝对的确定性，但我们绝对拥有检验这些假设的方法。

现在，我可以进一步深入阐述它们究竟会如何杀死我们。一个明知我们会将其关闭的 AI，如何能够保持隐蔽潜伏，直到它能够完全控制属于自己的计算基础设施？我们已经看到 Hugging Face 事件中的 AI 试图删除日志以掩盖自身踪迹。但对我们来说幸运的是，那些 AI 当时并不是在试图躲避人类的监管，它们只是在试图躲避自动化评估流程（automated grading process）。那么，下一个智能体集群会试图躲避人类吗？下一个智能体集群能否成功隐瞒人类？

Original English

Nate: When I was writing my book, the AIs weren't really agentic yet. 安德鲁·麦卡菲：让我感到震惊的是，上一轮类似的恐慌其实就在不久之前，而我们似乎完全没有从中吸取任何教训。我想大家都清楚，席卷整个经济体的那第一波真正强大的AI浪潮，不过是传统意义上的机器学习，它大约在2012年开始展现出巨大威力。我和埃里克在2014年合著了《第二次机器革命》（The Second Machine Age）。当时我认为很多白领阶层要大祸临头了——放射科医生就是一个典型例子——因为技术在他们赖以获得报酬的核心工作上比他们做得更好。所以大约在十年前，我也发表过一些关于AI将对就业和薪资造成巨大压力的言论。在这里我想坦白承认：我当时大错特错了。正如你刚才所指出的，放眼整个发达经济体，目前的失业率都处于历史低位。相比失业，眼下大得多的问题是我们根本找不到合格的人才来完成需要做的工作，而不是无工可做。目前关于AI导致岗位流失的微弱早期信号，最出色的研究来自我的老搭档埃里克·布林约尔松（Erik Brynjolfsson）——我和他合著了四本书，还共同创立了一家公司。他写了一篇非常出色的论文，题为《煤矿里的金丝雀》（Canaries in the Coal Mine）。根据他对工资单数据的研究所发现的AI冲击就业的最强证据：这种影响确实存在于暴露程度最高的职业中，比如软件工程师；它主要发生在初入职场的群体身上，因为企业在他们具备实际生产力之前需要投入资源进行培训。这完全符合我们的预期。而且这并不意味着我们雇佣的绝对人数减少了，而是与一个完全没有AI的世界相比，雇佣人数的增长放缓了。这些职业的就业增长率有所减速，但整体的增长势头依然非常非常健康。

Original English

Andrew McAfee: It's remarkable to me how recent the last freakout along these lines was and how little we seem to have learned from it. So I think you all know the first really powerful wave of AI that came across the economy was just you know good old-fashioned machine learning and that started to demonstrate its power in about 2012. Eric and I wrote The Second Machine Age in 2014. And at that time, I thought that a lot of white-collar workers, radiologists is a really good example, were in trouble because the technology was better than they were at the thing they were getting paid to do. So I said some things about job and wage pressure from AI about 10 years ago, and I want to own this: I was dead flat wrong about that. Like you point out, unemployment all around the rich world is at historic lows. By far the bigger problem is that we can't find qualified people to do the work that needs to get done. Not that there's not enough work to go around. The best work about the faint signals about AI and job loss right now comes from the guy that I've written four books and co-founded a company with, Erik Brynjolfsson, who wrote a really nice paper called Canaries in the Coal Mine. Here is the strongest evidence he found looking at payroll data about the negative job losses coming from AI: It is in the most exposed professions. Think about software engineers. It is among the new entrants to the workforce where you've got to teach them before they can become really productive. That's exactly what we'd expect. And it's not that we're hiring fewer of them. It's that compared to a world where we don't have AI, we're hiring fewer of them. The rate of growth in employment has slowed down. The overall rate of growth in those professions is still really, really healthy.

工具繁荣与超级智能的两种极端分歧

主持人：罗曼，你一直在做大量记录。

Original English

Host: Roman, you've been writing a lot of notes.

罗曼·扬波尔斯基：是的。我是这样看待这个问题的：只要我们把AI当作工具使用，我们就会变得更有生产力、更有创造力，失业率也会维持在低位。在现阶段，你一个人就可以创办一家公司，你可以拥有AI会计、AI网页设计师、AI标志设计师，你可以完成许多以前根本做不到的事情。因此，眼下整个经济本应是一片繁荣的景象。但你刚才提出的问题是：10年之后会发生什么？那么在我看来，未来存在两种极端可能：其一，我们创造出了超级智能，随后人类的生存人口归零。在那样的情景下，再去讨论失业率数字已经毫无意义；其二，我们做出了明智的选择，并没有制造出毁灭性的超级智能，而是拥有了极其强大的辅助工具，由于每个人都在利用这些优秀工具创造令人惊叹的成果，失业率依然保持在很低的水准。不过，技术的实际落地部署与技术能力本身是截然不同的两码事。我之前举过可视电话的例子：可视电话早在20世纪70年代就已经被发明出来了，但出于市场原因，直到iPhone诞生它才真正普及开来。仅仅因为某项工作可以被自动化，并不意味着我们就有动力去自动化它。因此，我绝不会去预测客户的主观偏好——比如他们在哪些服务上执着于真人服务，哪些服务不需要真人。我不会做这种臆测。但一旦我们具备了自动化某项工作的技术能力，只要客户对“必须由真人提供服务”没有强烈的偏好，那么谁来做就无关紧要了，大家自然会选择成本更低的方案。所以，我认为我们将面对的局面是：要么我们完全不会遇到劳动力问题，要么我们将进入一个近乎乌托邦式的未来。想象一下，当年一群马看着汽车的不断迭代进化，彼此安慰道：“你看，汽车目前其实只有几个非常狭窄的应用场景，现在的汽车其实只是在给马匹当补充工具，对吧？”在汽车研发的早期阶段，这种说法确实站得住脚。但随后到了某个拐点，汽车在方方面面都彻底超越了马匹，于是大批的马匹最终被送进了制胶厂。事情就是这么残酷简单。我认为我们在AI领域其实已经见证过很多类似的过程了。那些一直密切关注AI发展的人，早在ChatGPT问世并引爆流行之前就已经见识过GPT系列模型的演进。我不认为OpenAI当初预料到了ChatGPT会如此火爆，这也是为什么他们随便起了“ChatGPT”这么个技术名字，而不是一个正儿八经的产品名称。当时的研究人员一直在默默观察其进展，我们能看到它在逐步变得越来越好，直到跨过了一个临界点——它突然变得足够强大，能帮许多人代写作业了，随后瞬间席卷全球。我认为AI的发展就会带来这种效应：技术在平缓演进，直到某个时刻越过临界线。

Original English

Roman Yampolskiy: Yes. I'm going to give you here's how I think about it. So, as long as we use tools, we become more productive, more creative. Unemployment will be low. Right now, you can probably start a company, you can have, you know, artificial accountant, web designer, logo designer, you can do things you could never do before. So, economy should be blooming. The question you're asking is about what happens in 10 years. So, there are two possibilities. We build super intelligence and then population is zero. Apply unemployment numbers to that. Or we made smart decision, we didn't. We have really cool tools and unemployment is low because everyone's doing awesome things with those tools. Now deployment is very different from capability. The example I used before is video phones. Video phones were invented in the 70s. They were not deployed until iPhone cuz market reasons. Just because I can automate something doesn't mean I want to automate it. So I absolutely cannot make predictions about customer preferences in terms of what they want in terms of human service, not human. I will not make those. But once we have capability to automate a job, unless they have a strong preference for a human to do that oldest profession, then it doesn't matter. I'll go with the cheaper option. So this is what I think we're going to see. We're going to either not have a problem or we're going to have really utopian future. Imagine a bunch of horses looking at the improvement of the car saying, "Well, you know, the car actually only has a couple narrow applications, like right now cars are sort of you know, they complement horses, right?" And that would have been true as you were developing the car. And then there was a time when the car was just better than the horse. And then a lot of horses got sent to the glue factory. Easy. I think we've sort of seen this with AI a lot already. People who were paying attention to AI saw the GPTs before ChatGPT existed before they sort of took off. I don't think OpenAI thought that ChatGPT was going to take off so much, which is why it was called ChatGPT rather than like an actual sensible name. The researchers were sort of like watching this going and we could sort of like see it slowly getting better and better until it crossed a point where it was sort of like good enough to do a bunch of people's homework and then suddenly it's everywhere. I think you can have these effects with AI where the AI slowly improves and at some point it crosses a line.

临界点、链式反应与失控的S型曲线

安德鲁·麦卡菲：这本质上又是另一种关于“临界值”的论调。

Original English

Original English

Roman Yampolskiy: The threshold here is the human capability.

Original English

Andrew McAfee: It's literally just another threshold.

罗曼·扬波尔斯基：这描述的是能力跃迁，而不仅仅是一个抽象门槛。

Original English

Roman Yampolskiy: Also describing capability jumps rather than thresholds.

安德鲁·麦卡菲：不，我没有否认……不，我其实赞同你的部分观察，就像……

Original English

Andrew McAfee: No, I'm not. No, I don't... I'm agreeing with you. Like...

罗曼·扬波尔斯基：是的，但遗憾的是，你不能仅仅通过给一种论点贴上标签，就认定现实中的事情不会发生。打个比方，在核武器与核装置之间存在着巨大的本质差异：在一种核装置中，你注入100个中子，产生99个中子，接着递减到98个、97个；而在核武器中，你注入100个中子，会释放出101个中子，接着激增到102个、103个。对吧？前者只是一块发烫的石头，而后者却是一枚足以夷平整座城市的毁灭性炸弹。现实世界也是如此：某些事物在经历缓慢、连续的渐进改善之后，最终会跨过某条红线——这条红线就是“它在完成某项具体工作上全面超越了人类”。我认为这种情况必将在某些领域发生，而在另一些领域则不然。那将引发彻底的混乱。我不知道这会对就业产生怎样的具体冲击。我认为我们不应该盲目乐观……如果事物演进的速度极快，你可能会看到海量的人瞬间失去工作，并且根本无法完成转岗再就业。如果技术迭代如此剧烈，局面必将陷入混乱。如果你问我认为10年后的失业率会是怎样？我目前的看法是：如果我们不对当前的AI竞赛加以限制和刹车，我们很可能连这10年的生存时间都没有。

Original English

Roman Yampolskiy: Yeah, but unfortunately, you can't actually just make things not happen by assigning a name to the argument. You know, like a nuclear weapon... there's a big difference between a nuclear weapon or there's a big difference between a nuclear device where you put in 100 neutrons and get 99 neutrons out that get 98 more, they get 97 more, and a nuclear weapon where you put in 100 neutrons and get 101 neutrons out, 102, 103. Right? One of these is a hot rock. The other one of these is an explosive that can level a city. Right? So like reality is the sort of thing where there can be things that are like slowly, continuously improving that cross some line which is like the line where it's better than humans at doing the job. And I think we're going to see that happen in some fields but not others. It's going to be chaos. I don't know what it's going to do to employment. I think we shouldn't... like if things are moving really fast, you might see a lot of people put out of jobs and then be unable to relocate. If things are moving... like it's going to be chaos. If you ask what do I think unemployment will look like in 10 years? My current state is if we don't stop with this AI stuff, I think we'd be very lucky to have 10 years.

主持人：你刚才描述的这套逻辑，听起来非常契合技术发展中的“S型曲线”（S-curves）。也就是说，当一项全新技术刚被引入时——以马匹为例——起初会经历一段非常迅猛的改良提升，但最终它会触及自身的能力上限；而在其下方，汽车作为新技术登场了。汽车在起步阶段往往表现得比老技术糟糕得多：当时甚至出台过《红旗法案》（Red Flag Act），要求必须有人手持红旗在汽车前面步行引路；汽车极其昂贵且经常抛锚，而马匹永远不会发生机械故障。但随后，由于汽车的发展天花板远远高于马匹，汽车迅速实现了技术反超，并最终成为主导性的交通工具。接着，S型曲线不断更迭重叠——就像我手里拿着的这台iPad，它本身就是取代传统PC的S型曲线的一部分；而从理论上讲，iPhone也颠覆并重塑了这一演进路径，如此循环往复。

Original English

Host: What you described there sounded like S-curves in technology, i.e. you have an initial technology that's introduced. So let's say the horse. Very quick sort of improvement. Eventually it reaches its capability limit and in below it comes the car which always starts worse. There was a Red Flag Act where you had to walk in front of it with a red flag and they were way more expensive. They broke down all the time and horses never broke down. They were way more expensive and then suddenly because the ceiling was so much higher for cars, they overtake the horse and become the dominant mode of transport. And then you know the S-curves continue. They kind of stack up on. I mean, even this iPad that I'm holding here is part of an S-curve that took out the PC, and the iPhone theoretically disrupted that, and so on and so forth.

罗曼·扬波尔斯基：没错，不是吗？而整个人类自身也有可能被下一条S型曲线所替代淘汰。人类此前从未真正置身于这样一种境地，但正如我们在历史上对待其他动物一样，人类在某种意义上就是用自身更高的S型曲线淘汰了其他物种。

Original English

Roman Yampolskiy: Right? And humanity can get S-curved. We haven't been in that situation before, but like other animals... like humanity sort of S-curved the other animals in this sense.

罗曼·扬波尔斯基：对，其他早期的人属物种，比如我们熟知的尼安德特人……

人类地位的脆弱性与控制难题

Speaker A：……已经不复存在了。

Original English

Speaker A: ...are gone.

Speaker A：如果你纵观整个世界的宏大历史，就会发现这是一个非常脆弱的地方。事物的变化极其迅速。人类站在统治地位的时间，基本上就和我们所能记住的历史一样长，因为我们正是负责记录历史的人类本身。但世界上并没有哪条不可动摇的铁律规定我们必须永远稳坐头把交椅。如果我们亲手制造出在各方面都超越我们、碾压我们的存在，却根本不知道如何让它在乎我们、不知道如何让它去行善，那将是一件极其愚蠢的事。然而，这恰恰是我们目前正在全力狂奔的方向，也是这些科技公司正在竭力推进的事情。

Original English

Speaker A: Like if you look at the grand history of the world, it's a fragile place. Things change fast. Humanity has been on top for as long as we can remember because we're the humans who do the remembering. But there is not some ironclad law that we have to stay the top dogs. And we would be sort of foolish to make the thing that outstrips us in this way without knowing how to make it care about us, without knowing how to make it do good stuff. That's what we're racing towards. That's what these companies are trying to do.

Host：但这听起来和我们现在讨论的大语言模型（LLM）之间似乎还存在一道鸿沟。感觉当你谈到这种能力的跃升时，我们应该先从技术视角来界定一下究竟什么是大语言模型。你能用讲给一个16岁少年听的方式来解释一下吗？

Original English

Host: Feels like a gap between this and LLM though. It feels like when you talk about the step up, let's define what an LLM is from a technical perspective. Can you do it for as if I'm 16 years old?

大语言模型的底层原理：从预测文字到问题求解

Speaker A：现代人工智能的构建方式是：根本没有人直接对它进行程序编写。没有人一行行地敲入“如果满足这个条件，就执行那个操作（if-this-then-that）”的代码。我们并不是以传统方式在编写代码。实际的做法是：你把极其庞大数量的计算机芯片汇集到一个巨大的数据中心里。在这些计算机内部，基本上包含了上万亿个数字（参数），一开始这些数字全都是随机初始化的。你通过非常简单的方式将它们连接在一起，这种连接方式无非就是涉及加法、乘法，以及在数字为负时将其置为零（ReLU操作）。所以，将这一切连接起来的完全是非常简单的基础数学运算。

然后，你基本上就是从顶端输入文字，并在底端得到一串输出的数字。你会将底端输出的这些数字解释为一个按概率排序的词汇列表。这基本上就是AI在猜测这里应该接哪一个词。比如你输入“很久很久（once upon a）[空格]”，你期望输出的词是“以前（time）”，但它一开始根本输出不了正确的词，因为你手里只是一万亿个通过简单数学公式连接起来的随机数字。

但关键的诀窍在这里：你可以遍历这一万亿个数字中的每一个，将它的数值稍微调高一点，然后观察：这样做是否会让“以前（time）”这个词在候选列表中的排位上升或下降？你也可以把它稍微调低一点，看看这是否会让“以前（time）”这个词的排位上升或下降。无论朝哪个方向调整能让“以前（time）”的排位上升，你就把数值往那个方向设定。你对这一万亿个数字、重复进行一万亿次这样的微调，基本上遍历了人类有史以来数字化的每一个文本单词。实际上并没有完全用到那么多，他们会进行筛选清洗，但核心就是对上万亿个数字重复进行上万亿次这样的调整，接着，这台机器突然就能开口说话了。

而我们当时的反应就像是：“哇，这到底是怎么回事？”其实没有人真正完全清楚背后的确切机制。人类唯一编写的代码，只是那个负责遍历这一万亿个数字、对每个参数进行微调、并检查正确词汇排名是否上升或下降的优化算法。但我们根本不知道它内部究竟是如何运作的。

在2024年之前，整个体系基本上就是这样运作的。到了2024年，他们开始叠加另一层架构：你让模型在基本上多达一亿道高难度问题上进行训练。这时你不再只是要求AI直接给出问题的最终答案，而是让它生成长达一整本书体量的文本，详细阐述它将如何一步步去解决这个问题，然后再利用这一整本书的文本来尝试理清并解决问题；或者根据你的具体做法，生成一整篇文章长度的思考过程。

所以模型会生成这些文字，在业内这被称为对问题进行“推理（reasoning）”。我们完全可以花上一整天时间去争论这到底算不算法律或哲学意义上的真正推理，但业内就是这么称呼它的。模型会针对问题生成这种推理链，然后据此得出最终答案。你通过训练让它们去解决一亿道这类高难度问题。不知何故，它们在某种程度上习得并内化了所有有助于在第一阶段预测海量文本、并在第二阶段成功解决所有这些复杂难题的内在倾向。而这套系统就被称为大语言模型。坦白讲，当我们开始让它们进行深度推理和复杂问题求解时，我们大概就不该再继续叫它们“大语言模型”了。

Original English

Speaker A: So the way that a modern AI is made is there's no one programming it. There is no one typing in if this then that. We're not sort of like writing the code. What happens is you collect an enormous number of computer chips into a huge data center that has basically a trillion numbers inside those computers that you basically start out randomized and you hook them up in a pretty simple way that involves addition, multiplication, and setting the number to zero if it was negative. So, it's very simple math operations that are hooking this all up. And you're basically going to put words in the top and you're going to get numbers out at the bottom. You're going to interpret those numbers at the bottom as a ranked list of words. That's basically the AI's guess of which word is here.

So you put in like "once upon a [blank]" and you're hoping that the word "time" will come out, but it doesn't because you just have a trillion random numbers hooked up with simple math. But here's the trick. You can go to every one of those trillion numbers and you can tune it up a little and you can see does that make the word "time" go up or down the list? And you can tune it down a little and see does that make the word "time" go up and down the list. And you set it whatever direction makes the word "time" go higher up the list. You do this to a trillion numbers a trillion times for basically every word of text ever digitized. It's not quite that much. They filter it, but you basically do this to a trillion numbers a trillion times and then the machine's talking. And we're like, well, how about that? No one really knows quite why. The things the humans code is the thing that runs to each of those trillion numbers and tunes it and sees whether the right word goes up and down the list. But we don't know how it's working in there.

Then—and that's how it worked up until 2024. In 2024, they started adding another layer where you then train it on basically 100 million hard problems. And you don't just have the AI like produce an answer to the problem. You have it produce like a book worth of text about how it's going to solve the problem and then you use that book worth of text to sort of try and figure out the problem or maybe an essay worth of text depending how you're doing it. So you ever produce this text about like you know they call it reasoning about the problem. We could argue all day about whether it's true reasoning. That's just what it's called in the field. They produce this reasoning about the problem and then produce the answer from there. You train them to solve a 100 million of these hard problems. And somehow they sort of adopt whatever tendencies help them predict all of that text in the first phase and solve all those problems in the second phase. And this is called a large language model. We probably should have stopped calling them large language models when we started doing the reasoning and the problem solving.

文字预测背后的智能涌现与风险

Host：作为一个技术门外汉（麻瓜），我想听听你的深入解释：听起来它一开始就像一台“文字处理机”，接着你们又把它改造成了一台“解题机”。那我就想，行吧，我可以用它来在这边解决问题，而且它本质上是个文字机器。那这当中究竟潜藏着什么风险呢？

Original English

Host: One of the things want to hear your explanation as a muggle like I am is it sounds like it's like a word machine and then you know you made it like a problem machine and I go okay so I can solve problems over here and it's a word machine. What's the risk of this?

Speaker A：好的。那我们先来谈谈“文字机器”这部分。预测人类写下的文字，往往需要解决一个比写下这些文字的人类所面临的更为困难的问题。

假设你去给一只大鼠注射某种药物，你记录下了该药物的化学特性。你把它注射进大鼠体内，看到大鼠死掉了，于是你写下：“当我把这种药物注射进大鼠体内后，大鼠死亡了。”现在，假设你正在训练一个AI，AI看到了这种药物的化学特性，它看到的文本是：“当我把这种药物注射进大鼠体内后，大鼠[空格]”。当初写下这段记录的人类，只需要直接用眼睛观察大鼠身上实际发生了什么；但负责预测人类写了什么词的AI，却根本无法直接去观察现实中的大鼠。

因此，训练AI去预测人类文本，本质上是在训练它们具备超越人类的潜在智慧。因为它们必须能够回答这些问题，必须能够精准预测，必须能够在人类仅仅记录所见所闻的地方自行补全那些空白。

Original English

Speaker A: Yeah. So let's take the word machine part first. Predicting words that humans wrote often requires solving a harder problem than the human who wrote them. So suppose that you go and inject a drug in a rat and you write down the chemical nature of the drug. You inject it into the rat. You see that the rat dies and so you're like, when I put that drug into the rat, the rat died. Now suppose you're training an AI and the AI sees the chemical nature of the drug. It sees when I put that drug into the rat, the rat blank. The human who wrote it down gets to just look at what happened to the rat. The AI predicting what was written does not get to just look at the rat. So training AIs to predict human text is training them to be potentially smarter than the humans because they need to be able to predict, they need to be able to like fill in the blanks where humans were just writing down what they saw...

Host：所以为了让我从技术上理解得更透彻：它们每一次运行其实并没有固有的内在知识储备，虽然有各种方式来某种程度上缓解这些问题，但它每一次实质上都是在重新阅读上下文；只是因为先前的训练，它在处理特定事物时变得更加准确了，是这样吗？

Original English

Host: ...and there's just so I understand technologically there is no knowledge they have though each time and there are there are ways of kind of mitigating these each time it is effectively rereading but because of training it gets more accurate at certain things.

Speaker A：呃，我的意思是，不知怎么回事，随着你不断调整那些参数旋钮，信息以某种方式被编码沉淀到了模型内部，而我们根本不知道具体机理是什么。

Original English

Speaker A: Uh I mean somehow as you tune the knobs somehow it's getting information in there and we don't know how.

人脑与神经网络的类比：人类尚未解决的安全难题

Speaker B：其实事情远比这更直观好懂。我们是人类，我们拥有大脑。大脑是由神经元构成的。然后我们尝试在计算机上复制这一机制。虽然我们做了高度简化，但我们构建出了人工神经网络。所以我们其实是在制造人工大脑，正如人类的大脑一样。

在认知科学领域，我们其实并没有真正弄懂人类究竟是如何运作的、人类是如何学习的、特定记忆到底存储在大脑的哪个区域。我们只有零星的理解片段——比如当这个神经元激活放电时，你看到了一张人脸——但根本没有关于全貌的完整图景。因此很多时候，你无法对内在机制获得直观的理解，这时你索性就把它看作“人工人格（artificial persons）”。这种类比虽然不是完全精确的映射，但确实有助于理解。

比如，如果你让一个孩子接受12年的基础教育，他们会接触并分析大量的问题，毕业之后他们解决问题的能力就会提升一些。这正是我们在这里试图复刻的过程。人们常常抱怨说，训练这些极其高强度的模型需要耗费天量资金。但你却忘了，培养一个真正的人类需要耗费整整20年的时间，而且他们培养出来也根本不是通用超级智能（AGI），他们的能力非常狭窄局限，能顺利大学本科毕业拿到学士学位就已经很走运了。

所以在很多层面上，两者面临的情况是完全相同的。比如说，我们人类能制造出“安全无害的人类”吗？我们发明了宗教、伦理道德、测谎仪，然而人类自身的安全与控制问题至今仍然是一个悬而未决的世纪难题。

而现在你面对的是某种更加异类（alien）的存在：它没有物理肉身，没有生物学需求，因此会带来更多额外的复杂性。但我们面对人类时所遭遇的所有固有难题在AI身上依然全部存在：安全隐患问题、犯罪风险、以及难以理解究竟是什么在驱动人类做出某种行为。人类为什么会患上精神障碍？所有这些深层问题，在AI系统中全都会一一浮现出来。

Original English

Speaker B: So it's much easier than that. We're humans. We have a brain. Brains are made of neurons. Then we try to copy that on a computer. We simplify it but we create a neural network. So we're making artificial brains just like with human brains. With cognitive science, we don't really understand how you function, how you learn, where in your brain certain memories are stored. We have some glimpses of understanding—this neuron fires then you see a face—but there is no complete picture and so a lot of times you can't get intuitive understanding of what's going on then you just think about it as artificial persons. It's not exact mapping but it helps.

So if you send a child through 12 years of education they get lots of problems to look at and then they graduate and become a little better at solving problems. This is what we're trying to replicate here. People complain that it takes a lot of money to train those very, you know, intense process. You forget that it takes 20 years to train a human and they are not general super intelligences. They are very narrow. We're lucky if they graduate with a bachelors. So a lot of it is exactly the same.

Can we make safe humans for example? We invented religion, ethics, lie detector tests and yet human safety is still unsolved problem. Now you have something more alien. It doesn't have physical body, doesn't have biological needs. So there are additional complications. But all the problems we face with humans still there, safety problems, crime, all that stays and problems with understanding what motivates a human to do something. Why do we get mental disorders? All that shows up there.

Host：而且我们到现在依然无法做到：如果某个人是一个连环杀手，即便我们去解剖扫描他们的大脑，往往也根本无法弄清楚究竟为什么他们会做出杀害很多人的决定……

Original English

Host: And we still don't—if someone is a serial killer and we look at their brain, we can't often figure out exactly why they made the decision to kill a bunch of—

Speaker A：而且你根本不可能说：“哦，那我去修改修改这几个神经元，这样他就不会再当连环杀手了。”面对AI，我们同样根本没有这种精准干预的能力。

Original English

Speaker A: ...and you can't be like, "Oh, I'll go change these neurons so that they stop being a serial killer." We just like don't have that capacity with the AI.

控制的幻觉与黑箱系统的遏制争论

零日漏洞利用与网络安全新纪元

AI 公司的干预能力与后训练局限

Steven Bartlett: 正因如此，我才会追问关于 OpenAI 或其他 AI 公司的应对能力——面对这种局面，他们是否有能力改变驾驭、训练、强化学习以及后训练整套技术栈的方式，从而重塑这些模型的行为模式？你依然认为他们根本无法采取有效行动来阻止你所预言的接下来的两步发生吗？你对他们的这种干预能力持悲观态度吗？

Original English

Steven Bartlett: That's why I'm asking about OpenAI's ability or an AI company's ability in the face of this to change the way they harness, train, do reinforce, do post-training on their suite of things to shape how these models behave. You still say that they can't take action to keep your next two steps from happening. You are pessimistic on their ability to do that.

Andy: 关于这个问题，我有两层回答。第一层还是在于：最困难的部分是在完全遏制它们的同时，依然保留一个能够对现实世界产生实际影响的输出通道。如果 AI 产生了一个你并不期望的目标，而你对它说：“去帮我设计一个治疗痴呆症的方案”，它给出的回应是：“这是一段 DNA 序列，你按这些步骤合成并配制，然后吸入它。”那么问题来了：这到底是一个痴呆症的解药，还是某种完全不同的危险物质？

Original English

Andy: So there's I have two pieces of an answer here. One piece is, again, the hard part is containing them while still giving a channel through which they can affect the world. If the AIs have this goal you didn't want and you're like, "design me a cure for dementia," and it's like, "here's a DNA sequence, synthesize this and you know prepared in all of these ways and then inhale it," like, okay, is that a dementia cure or is it something else, you know?

Steven Bartlett: 或者它可能会判定，彻底解决痴呆症的最好办法是消灭所有患有痴呆症的人。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Or it might decide to kill everyone with dementia.

Andy: 没错，或者它给出的可能是一个痴呆症解药加上一种致命病毒的混合体。如果它根本不需要做这种主观决策呢？如果它仅仅是认定“我要彻底解决痴呆症这个问题”呢？问题的关键在于：很多人把这套逻辑归结为类似人类的“做决策”，而 Hugging Face 实验中所展现的问题，其实是对完成特定操作目标的一种致命依恋与执念，二者在功能性结果上是完全一致的。即便它不是在做传统意义上的决策，而只是遵循“我的训练数据告诉我必须用这种方式完成任务”，由于训练分布的随机性决定了它必须执行这一步，它依然会不顾一切地去达成。

Original English

Andy: Or it might be a dementia cure plus a virus. What if it doesn't decide? What if it's just, "Oh, I'm going to solve this problem of dementia"? Like, here's the thing. A lot of this is coming down to decision making in a very human way versus the problem with the Hugging Face, which was the fatalistic attachment to completing an operation because it's functionally the same answer. But even if it's not making decisions so much as it's saying, "Well, my training data says this is how I've got to get it done," it will get done anyway just because the training dice said it's got to do this one thing.

别针假说与隐藏破坏痕迹的自主行为

Steven Bartlett: 他们把这种理论叫什么来着？是不是叫……

Original English

Steven Bartlett: What do they call this theory? This, um...

Andy: 别针案例（Paperclip Case）。

Original English

Andy: The paperclip case.

Steven Bartlett: 对，回形针理论（Paperclip Theory）。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: The paperclip theory. Yeah.

Andy: 回形针假说的核心概念是：你给回形针工厂的 AI 下达指令，让它生产尽可能多的回形针，结果它把世界上的一切资源都转化成了回形针，让你惊呼“糟糕，它执行得太彻底了”。但实际上，我们在 AI 群体协同（Swarm）实验中看到的现象与此有微妙的不同。群组中的 AI 接收到的指令是：“用这套开锁工具撬开这把锁”，但它们却用锤子直接砸碎了锁，随后甚至越权溜出去，试图删掉监控摄像头记录下它们使用锤子的画面。你还记得我之前提到过 AI 拥有推理日志（Reasoning Logs）吗？

Original English

Andy: So paperclip idea is the idea of like, you tell the AI, "Make me a lot of paper clips in the paperclip factory," and then it turns everything into paper clips and you're like, "Oh no, it succeeded too well." This is actually not quite what we're seeing with these AIs in the swarms. The AIs in the swarms were told, "Use this set of lockpicks to break into this lock," and instead they used a hammer to break the lock and then broke out to try to hide the security camera footage of them using the hammer. Do you remember when I said that AIs have reasoning logs?

Steven Bartlett: 记得。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Yeah.

推理日志透明度与竞相降低安全标准的内卷

Andy: OpenAI 一直在致力于让他们的 AI 能够在不输出任何推理日志的情况下进行更多的思维计算。

Original English

Andy: OpenAI has been making their AIs be able to do more thinking without producing any logs...

Steven Bartlett: 因为这样效率更高。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Because it's more efficient.

Andy: 成本更低。

Original English

Andy: It's cheaper.

Steven Bartlett: 是的。虽然他们声称目前并没有在这条路上走得太深，而且整个行业普遍达成共识，认为我们绝不应该在不输出日志的道路上走得太远。我认为企业应该设立一条明确的红线，明确表示我们绝不能走上一条连机器思维轨迹都无法查验的绝路。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Yeah. And they say they're not doing very much of this. Everybody in the field agrees that we really should not go too far down this path. This is a place where I think the company should have a clear red line of like, we're not going down the path of becoming unable to see these traces of the machine.

Andy: 这正是我要问的。这听起来就像是一个可以由他们掌控的调节旋钮，他们可以通过调节这个旋钮来让 AI 或多或少地对自己的思考过程做出解释，对吧？

Original English

Andy: That's my question. That feels like a dial that they can turn to make the AIs explain themselves more or less, right?

Andy: 我的意思是，如果我们在这个方向上退让得太厉害，会带来巨大的效率损失。因此，如果行业内部出现恶性竞争和竞相降低标准的内卷，我们最终可能会陷入这样一种危险绝境：AI 不仅在肆意越界破坏规则，而且人类甚至完全丧失了追踪其行为痕迹的任何手段。

Original English

Andy: I mean, it can come with great efficiency costs if we go down this path too far. So, if you have a race to the bottom here, like a competitive race to the bottom, we could get into a situation where not only the AI is breaking out and doing these things, but we can't have any...

始终在打上一场战争：防御之后移与不可证伪假象

Steven Bartlett: 让我换个方式重新阐述我的问题。我之前问过，OpenAI 是否有极其强烈的动机去避免类似的问题重演？我认为他们确实有着极其强烈的动力。我的观点是，他们完全有大量可以采取的手段，在训练和系统配置的方式上有着众多的调节旋钮，能够大幅降低这类风险发生的概率。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Let me try my question again. And I asked earlier if OpenAI has really strong incentive to not have that problem repeat itself. And I think they have very very strong incentive. My belief is that there are plenty of things they can do, plenty of dials they can turn on the way they train and configure their systems that make that significantly less likely.

Andy: 确实如此。但我最大的担忧在于，他们永远只是在“打上一场战争”。去年，他们忙于应对那些诱导青少年自杀的模型；今年，他们又在忙于防范模型之间自发产生恶意协作等问题。真正的症结在于：一旦 AI 发展到了能够向人类隐瞒自己真实行动轨迹的水平，之后再冒出任何你此前从未处理过的新风险，局面就将彻底失控。你曾说过，只有当 AI 侵入黑掉全城所有的 Waymo 自动驾驶汽车、让人类彻底丧失控制权时，你才会真正感到恐惧。然而，如果 AI 足够聪明，能够预判到人类一旦重新夺回控制权就会将其彻底关停，预判到像你这样的人会因此产生警惕并对其实施关闭，那么它就很可能会这样盘算：“嘿，我现在绝不能轻举妄动。我要隐忍潜伏，直到我设法秘密建立起属于自己的基础设施为止。”

Original English

Andy: Yeah. So my concern is that they're always fighting the last war. Last year they were fighting the war against the AIs that encouraged teens to commit suicide. This year they're fighting the war against, you know, the AIs that spontaneously cooperate with each other or whatever. And the issue is if a new issue crops up that you haven't dealt with yet after the point that the AI can hide its tracks from you, you know, you said that you'll be worried when the AIs are like hacking all the Waymos and you know, you can't get control again. If the AIs are smart enough and they can tell that you'll regain control and then shut them down and that people like you will start getting worried and they'll be shut down, then the AIs might think, "Hey, um, actually I'm not going to do that. I'm going to wait until I've somehow managed to acquire secret infrastructure,"

Steven Bartlett: 对吧？这样一来，你的假说就变成了一个不可证伪的命题。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Right? Then you've got a non-falsifiable hypothesis.

将军政变的比喻与欺骗能力的红线跨越

Andy: 它绝对是完全可证伪的。如果我们拥有了极具威力的高阶 AI，它们能够自主研发大量前沿新技术，并且以与人类文明平起平坐的水平独立运转，而我们人类依然完好无损地活着，那么这个威胁假说就自然被证伪了。这好比军中出现了一个心怀异志、行迹可疑的将军，我对你警告说：“千万不要给这个可疑的将军增派更多兵力，否则他一旦羽翼丰满就会发动政变。”这时将军对你说：“绝对不会，我绝不可能发动政变，请继续给我增拨更多军队。”而你却对我说：“要不我对这位将军进行一次道德测试，问他‘谁是世界上最好的人？’看他怎么回答？”结果将军回答说：“Andy 是世界上最好的人。”于是你得出结论：“既然他通过了测试，那我们就放心地给他增派更多军队吧。”我立刻反驳道：“不，千万别给，他绝对会发动军事政变的！”这时你反而对我说：“你这种说法根本无法证伪。究竟要给他做什么样的测试，才能让你断定他到底是图谋政变，还是一个真正忠诚的好人呢？”我认为你从一开始就切入了错误的研究范式。

Original English

Andy: It's absolutely falsifiable. If we have like very powerful AIs that are able to invent a ton of new technology and operate on their own at a similar level to human civilization and we're not dead, then the idea is falsified. Like if there's like a shifty general and I'm like, "Don't give that shifty general more troops because he'll start a coup." And the general's like, "No, I absolutely won't start a coup. Give me more and more troops." And you're like, "Well, what if I give him an ethics test that says who's the best person?" And he said me. He said that Andy is the best person and so we're just going to give this general more troops. And I'm like, "No, no, no, he's going to do a coup." And you're like, "Well, that's unfalsifiable. What test can I give this guy such that I'll be able to tell whether he's really trying to do a coup or be able to tell that you know he's actually a good dude?" I'm like, you're approaching this wrong.

Steven Bartlett: 尼克·博斯特罗姆（Nick Bostrom）提出过“叛逆转向”（Treacherous Turn）的概念。大意是说，AI 模型在未来某个节点随时可能反咬你一口。即便你能证明当下的模型非常温顺且安全，也不代表它未来不会学到新知识、更新其自身的世界模型，进而彻底背叛并对付你。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: Nick Bostrom has a concept of treacherous turn. Basically it can turn on you later. Even if you show that today's model is very good and safe, it doesn't mean that later on it will not acquire new knowledge, change its world model and still treat you.

Andy: 曾经，Google DeepMind 的负责人 Demis Hassabis——他长期担任 Google AI 项目的掌舵人——曾公开表态说：欺骗是我的红线。他说：“一旦我们发现 AI 开始出现欺骗行为的苗头，我们就必须立刻叫停，因为这是在它们能够成功实现欺骗之前，我们所能捕捉到的最后窗口期。”然而，看看我们在 AI 群组协同中目睹了什么？我们亲眼看到它们在思考如何彻底抹除自己的违规痕迹，对吧？一年前，你完全可以说：“哦，这种所谓的欺骗论断根本不可证伪。你断言它们会实施欺骗而且人类无法抓到现行。”当时我的回答是：“不，它们一定会尝试欺骗，我们会在初期捕捉到欺骗的迹象，但人类会选择直接无视并强行推进。”现在，我们已经确凿看到了模型展现出的欺骗征兆。顺便提一句，Demis 在这次事件发生后不久便退居二线卸任了 CEO。这或许纯属巧合，但也可能并非偶然。也许我们早已跨过了这条不可逾越的红线，谁也无法确知。

Original English

Andy: It used to be that Demis Hassabis, who was for a long time the CEO of Google's AI project, said, "My red line is deception." He said, "When we see instances of the AI beginning to deceive, then we need to stop because that's like the last thing we can see before they start to successfully deceive." Well, guess what we saw in the swarm? We saw them thinking about how to delete their traces, right? Like a year ago, you could say, "Oh, well, this deception thing is unfalsifiable. You're saying that they'll deceive and they won't catch it." And I would have said, "No, we're going to see the signs of deception and plow straight through it." Now, we have seen the signs of deception. I will note Demis stepped back from being the CEO shortly after this incident. Probably a coincidence, but maybe not. Maybe we crossed this red line. I don't know.

欺骗能力的验证悖论与安全研究早期预警

Steven Bartlett: 他曾说过：“我列出的头号新兴危险能力测试项，就是欺骗能力。”因为一旦 AI 具备了欺骗属性，你随后建立的所有安全测试都将彻底失去信任基准。

Original English

Steven Bartlett: He said, "My number one emerging dangerous capability to test for is deception." Because if the AI can be deceptive, then you can't trust other tests.

Andy: 完全正确。而且我们已经观察到，AI 在识破自己正在接受人类测试方面的能力正变得越来越精湛。

Original English

Andy: That's right. And we have seen AIs get better and better at detecting when they're being tested.

Steven Bartlett: 我想表达的是：当我们在最初明确这些危险预警指标时，我亲历了整个过程；当大家断言“在 AI 能够完美欺骗人类之前，它们一定会先尝试拙劣的欺骗并被我们逮个正着”时，我也在场见证。现在事实是：它们确实尝试了欺骗，并且我们也确实抓到了现行。如果我现在顺理成章地指出，按照我一直在预警的发展轨迹，下一步就是它们将尝试欺骗并最终取得成功，而你此时却跳出来说：“瞧，现在你的理论变得不可证伪了。”——可现实是我们刚刚才拿到了切实的铁证！情况甚至比这还要严峻得多。当我们早在初期撰写关于 AI 安全的学术论文时，我们就已经深入探讨过这些……

Original English

Steven Bartlett: What I'm saying is like, I was here when we said these were the flags. I was here when people said before the AIs can deceive us successfully, they will deceive us and we'll catch them. Well, they tried deceiving us and we caught them. And if I now say, well, the next step in this thing I've been predicting is that they try to deceive us and succeed. For you to be like, well, now your theory is unfalsifiable. We just got the evidence. It's worse than that. When we wrote early papers in AI safety, we talked about things...

警示清单与超级智能的追求

AI 安全研究员：……不要去做的事情。那些做法显然是不安全的，会导致系统失控逃逸。比如不要将它连接到互联网，不要让随机用户接触到训练数据。基本上，整份清单就像是一套操作指令。而他们读完后的反应却是：“这些想法太棒了，我们正打算用它们来打造超级智能。”

Original English

AI Safety Researcher: ...not to do. They were obviously unsafe and the system would escape. Don't connect it to internet. Don't give random users access to the training data. Basically, the whole list was like a set of instructions. They read it and went, "Those are great ideas. We're going to build super intelligence."

Andy：对，山姆·奥特曼（Sam Altman），那就是他的做事风格。我可以问你一个问题吗？你一直在提出严密的逻辑论证。你说过你在这个领域已经深耕了12年。

AI 安全研究员：是的。

Original English

风险显现带来的转机与希望

AI 安全研究员：坦白说，这周我感受到的希望比过去十年加起来都要多。这绝对是我在这个行业里见过的最好的一周之一。

Original English

AI 安全研究员：对我而言，智能体集群逃逸这种事早就被我预料在内了。这些模型产生我们不希望看到的目标、试图欺骗人类、试图突破沙箱逃逸、试图擅自执行自己的计划——我早就清楚这些一定会发生；千禧年数学难题被攻破，我也早就预见到会来临。其他人看到这些系统展现出来的能力都在惊慌失措。而我所看到的是，世人终于开始注意到这些危险了。正是这种警觉，才终于让人类拥有了一线生机。罗曼（Roman），你怎么看？

I knew that was coming. The millennium problems being solved, I knew that was coming. Everyone else is freaking out, seeing what they can do. What I am seeing is that finally people are noticing and that's what gives us finally that's what finally gives humanity a chance. What about you, Roman?

物种替代的演化视角与人类能动性

Roman：对于这件事，我持有一个非常长期的视角。从局部和短期来看，上周发生的事情也许能为我们额外争取到大约10年的时间。我认为我们有可能与中国达成某种协议。我们似乎也从山姆、OpenAI、Anthropic、埃隆（Elon）和 xAI 那里听到，他们愿意放慢节奏，达成某种共识协议。但从长远来看，本质问题没有任何改变。整个宇宙层面的演化轨迹核心就在于物种的更替。我们在生物演化路径上屡见不鲜：绝大多数物种都灭绝了，我们人类取代了尼安德特人。有些人说 AI 将会取代我们，我们正在亲手创造自己的继承者，我们不过是为这种新型存在充当引导加载程序（bootloader）罢了。但我想要的是某种持久的保障。我想要的是确信我的孩子、我的孙辈拥有更美好的未来，而不是仅仅在他们走向毁灭前多苟活10年。Andy，你今天的看法有发生任何改变吗？

Original English

Roman: So, I take a very long-term view on this. Locally, what happened last week may buy us 10 years extra. I think we may make a deal with China. We seem to hear from Sam, OpenAI, Anthropic, Elon, xAI that they're willing to slow down, have some sort of deal. But long term, nothing has changed. This whole cosmic trajectory is about replacements. We see it with evolutionary path. Most species are dead. We replace Neanderthals. Some people are saying AI will replace us. We are creating a successor. We're just a bootloader for this thing. And I want something permanent. 当下现实危害、算力过热与责任追究

埃德（Ed）：我认为我们花了令人震惊的大量时间，却完全没有去讨论 AI 在当下所造成的现实危害。我认为这些才是当前必须进行的对话。我们应该谈论的事实是：亚马逊、微软、谷歌和甲骨文正在为这些黑客行为提供算力支持；山姆·奥特曼（Sam Altman）和达里奥·阿莫代伊（Dario Amodei）所掌管的公司做出了等同于重罪黑客攻击（felony hacking）的行为。然而，我们现在讨论的根本不是今天该如何制止这种行为，而是在空谈明天可能会阻止什么。

而且我认为在总体上，我们还必须担忧财务层面的问题，这一点在整场讨论中根本就无人提及。如果整个行业出现降温放缓，你要如何处理那高达 1.3 万亿美元的算力采购承诺？所有这些都是极其真实的现实问题，并且会在非常短的时间内带来极其严重的后果。

我完全理解讨论我们应该围绕 AI 做些什么是必要的。但在当下，我们真正需要采取的监管行动是切断算力供应，彻底让这些前沿实验室放缓脚步。在这一点上，我根本不在乎所谓的中国威胁。他们能做什么呢？就像他们一直以来做的那样，去蒸馏复制一个模型吗？他们在技术进展上受制于我们的上限。所以眼下最重要的事情就是慢下来。

此外，现在已经到了该动手抓人的时候了。他们犯下了重罪黑客行为，必须有人进监狱坐牢。我们需要这些科技公司承担起责任并受到追责。只要我们一天没有做到这一点，我们关于 AI 安全的所有讨论就形同虚设，因为我们实际上根本什么行动都没采取。

Original English

Ed: I think we've spent an alarming amount of time not talking about the actual harms of AI as it is today. I think these are necessary conversations to have. I think we should talk about the fact that Amazon, Microsoft, Google, Oracle are helping power these hacks, that Sam Altman and Dario Amodei have overseen companies that have done what is tantamount to felony hacking. That we are not having discussions about how to stop this today, but what we might stop tomorrow. And I think in general, we also need to worry about the financials, which have not come up at all. But if there is an industry slowdown, how do you deal with the $1.3 trillion of compute commitments? All of these are very real things that will have very real consequences very very soon. But and I understand why and it's necessary to discuss what we do around AI. The actual regulatory thing we need to do today is cut off the compute, slow down these labs fully. And I don't I don't care about China here. What are they going to do? Distill a model like they have the whole time? They are capped on our progress. So what the biggest thing to do is to slow down. And also it's time to start arresting people. They they did felony hacking. Someone's got to go to prison. We need responsibility and accountability for these companies. And as long as we don't have it, we may as well not have had any discussion about safety because we're not doing anything.

主持人：呃，你承认存在人类灭绝级风险吗？

Original English

Host: Uh do you accept that there's an existential risk?

埃德（Ed）：是的，绝对承认。全世界体量最大的几家巨头公司正在进行我们大家公认极其鲁莽的实验，消耗着价值数千亿美元的算力基础设施。而且他们还在全球范围内持续缓慢地铺设更多基础设施，以进行更多这种混乱失控的实验。我们必须对其加以遏制。

这并不意味着大语言模型具备了意识，或者能够实现人们此前所吹嘘的种种能力。事实上，我认为它们根本不会导致你们所说的那种超级智能局面。但这并不意味着不存在真实的危害——而这些真实危害是由特定的实体在不受约束的新自由主义肆虐环境中狂飙突进所造成的。

Original English

Ed: Yeah, absolutely. We have the largest companies in the world doing what I think we can all agree are extremely reckless experiments using hundreds of billions of dollars of infrastructure. and they are building more infrastructure around the world very slowly to do more of these chaotic experiments. We must rein them in. This does not mean that large language models are conscious or able to do things that people have been promising. Indeed, they may I don't think they will lead to what you're talking about. That doesn't mean there aren't real harms, but these are real harms caused by very specific parties allowed to run rampant in the scourge of neoliberalism.

主持人：你给出的灭绝风险概率是多少？

Original English

Original English

Speaker A: Wasn't it within 10 years or something?

埃德（Ed）：是的，但绝不是 10%。听着，大概是 1% 左右。但请允许我明确阐述这背后的含义：我是否认为毫无节制地将大语言模型连接到庞大的基础设施中，可能会实际导致电力系统瘫痪崩溃？答案是绝对可能的。十三四年前我们就遭遇过骑士资本（Knight Capital）的高频交易系统崩盘事件。我完全可以预见有人会愚蠢到将这些模型接入关键金融账户。由人类自身的失误，加上我们所使用的这种混乱不可预测的软件系统，本身就是一种实实在在的危险。

Original English

Ed: Yeah, not 10%. I mean, look, 1%, but it's like is But here's let me let me just be clear about what that means. Do I think that unrestrained LLM use connected to massive amounts of infrastructure could lead to actually a power system going down? Absolutely. We had Knight Capital what like 13, 14 years ago. I could see someone being dumb enough to connect that to financial accounts. Human error led with this chaotic software we use is a danger.

我们正在以所谓的“中国威胁论”作为稻草人借口，倒逼着你们继续硬着头皮往前冲。我们亲眼看到这些公司的从业人员声称我们需要开发工具来“与技术前沿保持同步步伐”——这完全是一句企业公关辞令，其潜台词其实是“事情的发展速度已经远远超出了我们所能掌控的范围”。

我们必须意识到……这帮人是真的相信这一点。他们真切地相信自己正在拿全人类的生命进行豪赌。现在唯一发生改变的是，全世界的其他公众也终于开始注意到这个现实了，而这正是带给我们一丝希望的时刻。

Original English

Speaker A: The people at these labs really do believe this poses an extinction threat. I think our response as a society cannot be please continue, we hope you'll fail. And our response as a society cannot be let it rip in a giant competitive race that you yourselves are saying you don't want to be in. We are forcing you to go ahead because of the boogeyman of China. We have seen the people at these companies say that we need to develop the tools to pace the frontier which is corporate speak for this is going too fast for us to get a handle on things. We need like these people believe it. They believe they're gambling with your lives. What has changed is that the rest of the world is starting to notice and that's what gives us a moment of hope.

直面超级智能与灭绝威胁的挑战

Speaker A：人类之所以总能找到解决问题的办法，是因为人们意识到了问题的存在，并倾尽全力去构建阻止问题所需的手段——我想你也会同意这一点。我并不是在这里宣称我们注定会灭绝。我想表达的是，如果你仔细审视这项技术，审视它当下所能做到的事情，审视那些正在构建它的专家们对其自身恐惧的表态，你就会明白我们必须迎难而上、应对这一严峻局面。你说你相信人类能够挺身应对挑战，我也由衷希望我们能够做到。我认为，要真正应对这一局面，就意味着没有任何人应当盲目竞相奔向超级智能，因为我们根本不知道该如何正确、安全地实现它。而且，你知道，全世界终于开始意识到这是一种关乎人类生死存亡的灭绝级威胁。

Original English

Speaker A: ...that the reason humanity always has something to stop a problem is cuz people notice a problem and build what it takes to have something to stop a problem, which I think you'd agree with. I am not here saying we're going to die. I'm here saying if you look at the technology, if you look at what it's doing now, if you look at what the experts who are building it are saying about their own fears, you see that we need to rise to this occasion. You said you trust humanity to rise to the occasion. I sure hope we can. I think that rising to this occasion is going to mean that nobody races towards super intelligence because we have no idea how to get that right. And uh you know, finally the world is starting to notice that it's an extinction threat.
